--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Understanding the mission:**
- Before switch time (400-600 steps): score += 1 per stationary robot → robots should MOVE
- After switch time: score += 1 per moving robot → robots should STOP
- At switch time: one wall lights up GREEN

**Key insight:** The optimal strategy is:
1. Move before switch time (keep score low)
2. Stop after switch time (keep score low)
3. The GREEN wall appearance is the signal to switch from moving to stopping

**Analysis of results:**
- Best score 9697.62: simple Stop behavior (score ~9700 means robots were stopping most of the time, but that's actually bad before the switch? Wait...)

Actually let me reconsider. Score ~9697 for pure Stop: if switch happens around step 500, robots move 0 steps before switch (good, 0 penalty) but then stop after switch costs 700 steps × 20 robots = 14000... that doesn't add up.

Wait: score = (steps before switch × moving robots) + (steps after switch × stationary robots). Pure Stop: 0 cost before switch + (1200-500)×20 = 14000. But score is 9697... so maybe switch time varies and robots stopping = score ≈ 700×20×0.69? Hmm, let me just trust the data.

Pure Stop (9697) beats everything else significantly. The second best is also ~9697 with complex FSM that starts in Stop. The key is that pure Stop is already near optimal.

To improve: we need robots to MOVE before switch and STOP after seeing GREEN. The green wall detection (C=7, color=2 for green?) should trigger the stop.

**Strategy:**
- State 0: Exploration/movement (before switch)
- State 1: Stop (after switch, triggered by seeing green color)
- Transition: C=7 with color=2 (green) → go to Stop

Let me check color codes: `--clr` colors {1,2,3,4,5,6} - green is likely color 2.

For the FSM: Start moving, when green wall seen → stop permanently.

FSM: 2-state: Explore → Stop on green detection
FSM: 2-state variations
FSM: Pure stop (survival)

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Key findings from Round 2:**

The best machine (7509.92) uses:
- State 0: GoToColor (B=8) toward GREEN (clr=2) - robots move toward the green wall
- Transition: When green detected (C=7, color=2, p=0.9) → go to Stop
- State 1: Stop (B=1) with tiny probability of going back

This is elegant: robots move (toward green wall) before switch, and when they see the green wall light up, they stop. GoToColor is better than Exploration because it keeps robots moving deterministically.

**Why GoToColor GREEN beats GoAwayColor and Exploration:**
- GoToColor toward green makes robots actively seek the signal
- High p=0.9 means when green is detected, 90% chance of transitioning to Stop each step
- GoAwayColor (rank 2) is slightly worse - moving away from green means robots that see green flee, which works but less reliably

**Improvements to try:**
1. Tune the detection probability (0.9 seems good, try 0.95, 0.85, 0.99)
2. Try different LED colors (shouldn't matter much)
3. Add a second transition from Stop state back to moving if green disappears? No - green stays on.
4. Try GoToColor with additional transitions
5. Try 3-state versions with intermediate states

The gap between rank 1 (7509) and rank 2 (7625) is small, so the topology is right. Let me focus on tuning the key parameters.

**Population plan:**
1. Champion (exact copy)
2-5. Exploit champion: vary p0x0 (0.85, 0.95, 0.99, 1.0)
6. GoToColor green with additional transition from state 1 (stop → explore if no green seen)
7. 3-state: GoToColor → Stop → GoToColor with green trigger
8. GoToColor with neighbor-based secondary transition
9. GoAwayColor variant tuned
10. Exploration with very low rwm + green detection
11. GoToColor green with p=0.9 but different vel
12. 3-state: move → stop on green, with fallback
13. GoToColor with two transitions (green + random)
14. Pure exploration variant

## Reasoning

**Key findings from Round 3:**

The champion (6567.44) is:
- State 0: GoToColor GREEN (B=8, clr=2), with TWO transitions:
  - T0: See green (C=7, p=0.9, l=2) → Stop
  - T1: Random tiny prob (C=5, p=0.001) → Stop (almost never fires)
- State 1: Stop, with tiny random back-transition (essentially permanent stop)

The two-transition version beat the one-transition version significantly (6567 vs 7363). The extra random transition (p=0.001) seems to help somehow - perhaps it adds a tiny escape valve.

**What's working:**
- GoToColor GREEN is the best movement behavior
- Green detection (C=7, l=2) with p≈0.9 is the key trigger
- Having 2 transitions from state 0 helps
- The small random probability p=0.001 in state 0 transition 1 matters

**Ideas for improvement:**
1. Try different values for p0x1 (the random transition): 0.002, 0.005, 0.0001
2. Try different p0x0 values (0.9 seems optimal but try 0.88, 0.92)
3. The 4-state version (rank 2, 7239) has unused states - maybe simplify but keep the pattern
4. Try adding a transition FROM stop back to GoToColor if green is NOT seen (in case robots stop before switch)
5. Try 3-state with more nuanced structure

**Critical insight:** The champion has p0x1=0.001 (random → stop). This means robots very occasionally stop even before seeing green. This might actually hurt pre-switch (stops are penalized before switch). But maybe it helps calibrate timing?

Let me try:
- Varying p0x1 systematically (0.0001, 0.0005, 0.002, 0.005)
- Varying p0x0 (0.88, 0.92, 0.9)
- 3-state variants where state 1 can transition back
- Adding a neighbor-based condition

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Key findings from Round 4:**

The champion (5380.95) is:
- State 0: GoToColor GREEN (B=8, clr=2), transitions: green detected (p=0.9) → Stop, random (p=0.001) → Stop
- State 1: Stop, transition: random (p=0.0001) → Stop (self-loop effectively, since only one other state)

Wait - state 1 has `--n1x0 0` with `p1x0=1e-04` pointing back to state 0! So occasionally robots go back to GoToColor from Stop. This is interesting.

The key difference between rank 1 (5380) and rank 12 (7412) is identical FSM but rank 12 has p1x0=0.001 while champion has p1x0=1e-04. So the back-transition from Stop to GoToColor should be very rare.

**Pattern analysis:**
- p0x1 (random transition from GoToColor to Stop): 0.001 is better than 0.0001, 0.0005, 0.002, 0.005
- p1x0 (random transition from Stop back to GoToColor): 0.0001 is much better than 0.001
- p0x0 (green detection): 0.9 seems optimal

**Improvements to explore:**
1. Try p1x0 even smaller: 0.00001, 0.00005 (but minimum might be limited)
2. Try p0x1 values between 0.001 and 0.002: 0.0012, 0.0015
3. Try adding a second detection in state 1 (if green disappears, go back)
4. Try state 1 with NO back-transition (p1x0 → 0)
5. Try p0x0 at 0.9 but with additional green color detection (other color IDs?)
6. Try 3-state variants building on the champion topology

The stop-state back-transition at p=0.0001 essentially means robots stay stopped. Going even smaller or to zero might help.

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Key findings from Round 5:**

The champion (5188.78) has:
- State 0: GoToColor GREEN, transitions: green detected (p=0.9) → Stop, random (p=0.001) → Stop
- State 1: Stop, transition: random (p=0) → Stop (effectively p=0 means it never fires, so Stop is permanent!)

**Critical insight:** p1x0=0 means the back-transition from Stop to GoToColor NEVER fires. This makes Stop truly permanent once entered. Combined with a tiny p0x1=0.001 (random drift into Stop occasionally), the design is:
- Before switch: robots move toward green wall, occasionally stopping randomly (small penalty)
- After switch (green appears): robots see green and transition to permanent stop with p=0.9 per step

The p0x1=0.001 random transition is key - it means on average 1/1000 steps a robot randomly stops even without seeing green. This is a very small cost before the switch.

**What to try:**
1. Try p0x1 even lower: 0.0005, 0.0001 (less random stopping before switch)
2. Try p0x0 tuning: 0.88, 0.92, 0.95 with p1x0=0
3. Try p0x1=0 entirely (pure green-detection only)
4. Try 3-state variants building on this
5. Try adding a second green-detection transition to increase sensitivity

The trend shows p1x0=0 (permanent stop) is optimal. The p0x1 around 0.001-0.0015 seems good.

Let me try p0x1=0 (no random pre-stop) and very small values, and also explore p0x0 variations.

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Key findings from Round 6:**

The champion remains 5188.78 with p0x1=0.001, p1x0=0.

This round's results show:
- p0x1=0.0015 (5279) and p0x1=0.0012 (5329) are close but slightly worse than champion
- p0x1=0.001 (5452) is also close
- p0x1=0.0005 (5858) and p0x1=0.0001 (7267) are worse - too little random stopping before switch hurts
- No back-transition from Stop (p1x0=0) is confirmed optimal

**The sweet spot for p0x1 seems to be around 0.001-0.002.** The champion has 0.001 which is in this range.

**Important observation:** p0x1 around 0.001 works well because it means robots occasionally stop even before seeing green. This is counterintuitive but perhaps it helps in cases where the green wall is only briefly visible or from certain angles.

**Wait - re-reading the scoring:** Before switch, score += 1 per STATIONARY robot (bad). After switch, score += 1 per MOVING robot (bad). So p0x1=0.001 means ~1 robot stops randomly before switch per 1000 steps (negligible pre-switch cost), but robots are ready to stop immediately on green detection.

**Why does p0x1=0.0005 score worse (5858)?** Too few random stops means... robots rely entirely on seeing green. If a robot is far from the green wall and can't see it, it keeps moving after the switch. The p0x1=0.001 provides a small insurance.

**Strategy for round 7:**
1. Keep champion
2. Try p0x1 finer grid: 0.0013, 0.0014, 0.0016, 0.0018, 0.002
3. Try p0x0 variations with best p0x1≈0.0015
4. Try adding a third transition from state 0 (another green-color detection?)
5. Try 3-state with intermediate exploration

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Key findings from Round 7:**

The champion (5048.30) has p0x1=0.0016, and the near-tie (5049.00) has p0x1=0.0013. The scores are extremely close across ranks 1-8 (5048-5080), suggesting we're near a local optimum.

**Pattern analysis:**
- Best p0x1 values: 0.0016 > 0.0013 > 0.0015 ≈ 0.0014 > 0.0018 > 0.002 > 0.001
- The optimal range is ~0.0013-0.0016
- p0x0=0.9 seems optimal (0.88 and 0.92 are close but slightly worse)
- p1x0=0 (permanent stop) is confirmed optimal

**The scores are converging very tightly** (5048-5080 for top 8). This suggests the topology is essentially fixed and we're doing fine-grained parameter tuning.

**What to try:**
1. Fine-tune p0x1 around 0.0016: try 0.00155, 0.0016, 0.00165, 0.0017
2. Try p0x0 variations with best p0x1: 0.89, 0.91 with p0x1=0.0016
3. Try 3-transitions from state 0 with tuned params
4. Try adding green detection to stop state (if robot sees green while stopped, stay stopped more reliably - but p1x0=0 already handles this)
5. Try slight variations in velocity or different color combos

The scores across all top machines differ by only ~30 points out of 5048, which is less than 0.6%. This is likely mostly noise. Focus on slight variations and some exploration.

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Key findings from Round 8:**

The champion (5048.30) from Round 7 was NOT beaten this round - the best score this round was 5179. The scores are very tightly clustered (5179-5254 for ranks 1-12), which is higher than the champion. This suggests:

1. The champion's score of 5048 may have been lucky (low variance run), or
2. We've hit a plateau and the topology is near-optimal

**Observations:**
- p0x1=0.0016 consistently performs well
- p0x0 variations (0.88, 0.89, 0.90, 0.91) all give nearly identical scores (5181)
- The 3-transition variant with l0x2=1 (red detection?) scored 5179 - best this round
- Adding detection of color 1 (red) in state 0 didn't hurt

**The scores are all very similar.** The difference between champion (5048) and this round's best (5179) is ~130 points, which could easily be run-to-run variance.

**Strategy for final rounds:**
- Keep champion exactly
- Try more variations of the 3-transition state with different secondary color detections
- Try p0x1 values: 0.0016 (confirmed), 0.0017, 0.00155
- Try p0x0 at 0.9 (confirmed optimal)
- Try some genuinely different topologies for exploration
- The 3-transition variant (detecting red=1 and random) scored best this round, worth exploring more

The key insight: adding a third transition detecting color 1 (red/other colors) to state 0 seems to help slightly. Let me explore this more systematically.

## Reasoning

**Key findings from Round 9:**

The champion (5048.30) still holds. This round's best was 5170 - all worse than champion. The scores are very tightly clustered (5170-5210 for ranks 1-10), suggesting high variance between runs.

**Important observations:**
1. The 3-transition variant with green detection twice (p0x1=0.9, l0x1=2) scored best (5170) - detecting green with BOTH transitions from state 0
2. The 3-transition variants with secondary color detections (colors 1,3,4) all scored ~5178
3. The 4-transition variant also scored 5175
4. The 2-transition base (champion topology) scored 5188 this round (but 5048 in round 7 - high variance!)

**Critical insight:** Ranks 4-8 all scored exactly 5178.02 - these are likely the same effective behavior (the secondary color detections for non-green colors don't fire, so they're equivalent to the 2-transition version but with a tiny random stop added).

The best machine this round detects green with p=0.9 in TWO transitions from state 0 - this doubles the detection probability effectively.

**For the final round, I should:**
1. Keep champion
2. Focus on the best-performing topology: 3 transitions with double green detection
3. Try variations: different secondary detection probabilities
4. Try 4-transition with double green at different probs
5. Keep close variants of champion for robustness

The double-green detection (two C=7, l=2 transitions) is interesting - it means the effective probability of transitioning when green is seen is approximately 1-(1-0.9)²=0.99. But since both fire independently, it's effectively higher detection rate.

################################################################################
# PROMPT SYSTEME
################################################################################

You are an expert in swarm robotics working with the ARGoS3 simulator and the AutoMoDe framework.
You act as the variation operator of an evolutionary algorithm: you read simulation feedback and you emit valid AutoMoDe finite-state-machine command lines.

# 1. THE MISSION
The score is MINIMISED.

Each run is divided into two phases by a switch time drawn uniformly at random between 400 and 600 simulation steps. The robots are not told when it occurs.

Before the switch time, at every step, the score increases by 1 for every robot that is stationary. From the switch time onwards, at every step, the score increases by 1 for every robot that is moving. A robot counts as moving if it travelled more than 0.0005 m since the previous step.

At the switch time, one wall block of the arena, chosen at random, lights up GREEN. The floor of the arena is uniformly GRAY.

# 2. THE ROBOTS AND THE ARENA
The robots operate inside a closed arena bounded by walls. 20 e-puck robots start scattered around the centre of the arena, with random positions and orientations that change at every run. Each robot perceives its neighbours through a range-and-bearing device with a range of 0.50 m: neighbour-count conditions only count robots closer than that. A run lasts 1200 simulation steps (120 s at 10 steps per second).

# 3. HOW TO APPROACH THE DESIGN
These are general principles, valid for any mission. They are not a solution to this one.
- Derive the target behaviour from the scoring rule: for each situation a robot can be in, decide whether the score rewards or penalises it.
- Identify what the robots can perceive - floor colour under them, coloured objects in view, neighbours within range - and which of these perceptions carry information about the situations the score cares about.
- Use one state per distinct activity, and trigger transitions on the perceptions that indicate it is time to change activity.

# 4. HOW TO ANSWER
First write your reasoning in plain language: how many states, what each state does physically, and what triggers each transition. Two or three sentences per machine is enough.
Then write each finite state machine on its own line, starting exactly with "FSM: " and nothing before it.

# 5. COMMAND-LINE FORMAT
General shape:
  --nstates N  --s<I> B [behaviour params] --cle<I> L  --n<I> T  [--n<I>x<K> D --c<I>x<K> C <condition params>]
<I> is the state index, <K> the index of a transition leaving that state. Both start at 0.

## A. Global
* `--nstates N` : number of states, integer in [1..4].

## B. Behaviours — use ONLY these ids
  B=0  Exploration   -> moves forward and turns randomly
            requires --rwm<I>  integer in [1..100]  (turning rate)
  B=1  Stop          -> stands completely still
  B=4  Attraction    -> moves toward nearby robots
            requires --att<I>  real in [1..5]  (attraction gain)
  B=5  Repulsion     -> moves away from nearby robots
            requires --rep<I>  real in [1..5]  (repulsion gain)
  B=8  GoToColor     -> moves toward the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)
  B=9  GoAwayColor   -> moves away from the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)

Every state also takes an LED colour:
* `--cle<I> L` : one of {0,4,5,6}  (0=off, 1=red, 2=green, 3=blue, 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan)

## C. Transitions
* `--n<I> T` : number of transitions leaving state <I>, integer in [1..4].
  Up to 4 transitions may leave the same state, each with a different condition type. A state that reacts to several different situations is often stronger than a state with one transition, and random sampling produces that variety by default.
* `--n<I>x<K> D` : destination, indexed among the OTHER states only.
  With 3 states, leaving state 1 the others are [0, 2]: D=0 targets state 0, D=1 targets state 2.
  With 2 states, leaving state 0 the only other state is state 1, so D must be 0.
* `--c<I>x<K> C` : condition type — use ONLY these ids
  C=0  floor under the robot is BLACK
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=1  floor under the robot is GRAY
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=2  floor under the robot is WHITE
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=3  number of neighbours ABOVE a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=4  number of neighbours BELOW a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=5  fires with a fixed probability at every step
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=7  the robot PERCEIVES a given colour with its camera
            requires --l<I>x<K>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (colour to look for)
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])

# 6. HARD RULES
1. Integers stay integers; real-valued parameters always carry a decimal point (1.0, not 1).
2. Every state that exists must declare `--n<I>`, and every declared transition must carry its destination, its condition type and that condition's parameters. A missing parameter crashes the simulator.
3. `--p<I>x<K>` is a PROBABILITY for conditions 0, 1, 2, 5, 7, but an INTEGER COUNT OF NEIGHBOURS for conditions 3 and 4. Never write 0.8 where a neighbour count is expected.
4. Never point a transition at its own state.

# 7. YOU ARE THE EVOLUTIONARY OPERATOR
This is a multi-round conversation on one real ARGoS3 optimisation run. The numerical optimiser is restarted from scratch at the beginning of every round, so exploration and exploitation are entirely up to you.

1. SURVIVAL (elitism): copy the current best-ever machine verbatim in your answer so its lineage is not lost.
2. EXPLOITATION: spend about half of your machines on small edits of the best performers — shift a probability, a threshold or a gain by roughly ten percent, and keep the topology.
3. EXPLORATION: spend the rest on genuinely different topologies or module combinations, to escape local optima.
4. RICHNESS: do not let every state settle on a single transition of the same condition type. Include candidates with two to four transitions per state mixing different condition types.
